Ebola outbreak shows that the world is not ready for the next pandemic By Olivia Ndubuisi A woman walks past a poster about Ebola prevention in Mambasa, Ituri, Democratic Republic of Congo, 24 August 2026 (Keystone/EPA/Dieudonné Dirole) As Ebola continues to spread in the eastern Democratic Republic of Congo, the burning question at recent UN pandemic preparedness meetings at the UN General Assembly was: Is the world willing and able to pay for readiness? When the Marburg virus hit Rwanda in September 2024, the country’s No. 1 goal was stopping community transmission. Marburg, in the same family as the Ebola virus, is second among the top-four deadliest viruses in the world. More than 80 per cent of people who contract the disease die. The country tracked the source of the zoonotic disease, and using genomic sequencing, sequenced the virus. They discovered that the outbreak was closely related to a sequence that happened in an outbreak of Marburg in Uganda in 2014. Rwanda mounted an ambitious response by beginning contact tracing, mass testing and surveillance. Ultimately, 9.6 million people were screened by about 54,000 community health workers. From the diagnosis of the first case of Marburg to the deployment of the first shot of the experimental Sabin Vaccine Institute inoculation was 10 days. The case fatality rate for the outbreak stayed at 22 per cent, the lowest ever recorded for the virus. Two years on, across the border from Rwanda, the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), has entered Day 139 of its fight against a new strain of an Ebola outbreak, called Bundibugyo. Approximately 3,700 people have died since the outbreak was declared in mid-May 2026. The first encouraging signs of progress to fight it may be emerging. Rwanda’s response to the Marburg outbreak, hailed the world over, was possible because the country had spent years preparing for such an epidemic. But the DRC is trying to build responses in altogether different conditions: It is coping with a humanitarian crisis amid a war. So the burning question that dominated the UN’s pandemic preparedness meetings at the General Assembly high-level session last week was: Is the world willing and able to pay for readiness now? On the ground in DRC According to the WHO, from 31 August to 20 September, the cases of Ebola in the DRC declined by approximately 26 per cent at the epicenter, Ituri Province. However, in the same period, North Kivu Province, near Ituri, recorded a 73 per cent increase, evidence that the outbreak was evolving across the numerous affected areas. “Each of the seven affected provinces has its own transmission pattern. One of our greatest concerns is that too many people are still dying before reaching treatment,” said Dr. Marie Roseline Belizaire, the regional emergency director for WHO Africa. She was speaking by Zoom at a recent UN briefing on the Ebola outbreak in eastern DRC and emergency preparedness ahead of El Niño and other health emergencies in the region. “Every death in the community also increases the risk of further transmission among family members, caregivers, and others involved in burial practices, and although adults account for most confirmed cases,” Dr. Belizaire added. “Children are especially vulnerable.” On the ground in the DRC, she said that all response teams are working closely with communities to identify suspected cases quickly and to address the misinformation that is keeping people from seeking appropriate care. The bigger emphasis has been moving the response away from major urban centers into rural areas, where the most-worrying epidemiologic trends are occurring. The needs are enormous. Money, time and a plan “The problem is multifaceted,” Dr. Catherine Smallwood, a WHO incident manager for the Ebola response in North Kivu, said in the recent UN event. “There is the access one, and there’s also the issue of funding. We need partners. We need time, we need money, we have a plan, and if we really, truly want to be able to stop this epidemic in the next six months, we will need additional resources.” For both Rwanda and the DRC, the realities of fighting the Bundibugyo strain are vastly distinct. Rwanda’s health minister, Yvan Butera, spoke at an event during the UN high-level week hosted by the Pandemic Fund, which focuses on pandemic prevention, preparedness and response in low- and middle-income countries, and the governments of Armenia and Rwanda. The forum spotlighted how countries are investing their own resources to close critical preparedness gaps. Butera said that building a readiness-response plan cannot be done in the middle of a war and humanitarian disaster. The DRC’s effort to control the Ebola outbreak is still lacking an approved vaccine. Provisions of other essential health services are also being carried out under sometimes deadly circumstances. The services include maternal health, routine immunizations, treatments of malaria and other baseline conditions resulting from widespread displacement of civilians. The decades-long conflict in the eastern DRC involves the M23 militia from Rwanda fighting Congolese troops in competition for the area’s mineral wealth. The violence has made the Ebola outbreak hard to contain. As of 30 September 2026, data from the Congolese authorities confirmed a total of 3,901 deaths out of 8,067 confirmed cases in the DRC from the outbreak. It has surpassed previous records to become the largest Ebola outbreak in the history of the DRC, but it has not spread to Rwanda. In May 2026, it spread into neighboring Uganda through infected cross-border travelers, but its government halted transmission and was officially declared virus-free by the WHO in August 2026. Lessons from Covid-19 Six years ago, the Covid-19 pandemic devastated global health systems, and many countries have yet to fully recover. Mathematical modeling by the Center for Global Development, a nongovernmental organization focused on reducing poverty, suggests that the likelihood of another pandemic of the same scale as Covid within the next 25 years will hit nearly 70 percent. Many global health experts believe the odds are climbing closer to a full certainty. But the world moves on too quickly, said Wessam Mankoula, the acting head of Emergency Preparedness and Response Division for Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, or Africa CDC. “SARS is over and we forgot, Covid-19 came, we’ve forgotten the devastation,” Mankoula said in a panel on building resilience during last week’s UN General Assembly session. “We need to invest more to build resilient systems now. And to ensure sustainability of our efforts, in the DRC and everywhere else we have to first fight our serious case of pandemic Alzheimer’s.” Mankoula’s work involves generating resources for the Pandemic Fund to remedy the Ebola outbreak in the DRC. His team is aiming for a fast turnaround of financial pledges into disbursement. “In the first two months of the response in DRC, we had 10 to 20 per cent success moving from pledges to disbursement,” he said. “The impact was that the first response plan for DRC was $240 million and the second response plan was $1.3 billion. So when there is a delay in dispersing resources, the cost of responding to an emergency dramatically increases.” According to media reports, $2.9 billion has been raised so far, based on Africa CDC numbers. Success, Mankoula added, is when the CDC’s support is no longer needed because countries got their pandemic readiness done in “peace time.” The World Bank operates the Pandemic Fund, whose largest contributors are the US, the European Commission, Germany, Italy and Japan. “The World Bank and the Pandemic Fund’s priorities are clear. Invest early, invest together, and be ready to act,” Paschal Donohoe, the managing director and chief knowledge officer at the World Bank said at the General Assembly event. “We learned from the pandemic [Covid-19] that countries that depended on others for the tools that they needed to survive paid a price,” he added. Yet at another UN meeting on pandemic preparedness, the US last week rejected a UN declaration pledging global solidarity on pandemic preparedness. The US said it was “not in a position” to support the text. The European Union also had strong objections. The US also withdrew from the WHO in January, after dismantling USAID last year. “It would be extraordinarily difficult in almost any funding environment,” Dr. Cecilia Mundaca Shah, vice president of global health at the UN Foundation, said, referring to the current circumstances of fighting the disease amid funding pullbacks. “But it is fair to say that the crisis in the DRC unfolding alongside the present funding climate is definitely being felt.” For Africa, the next pandemic — which is surely coming, experts say — may show that the continent is not readier than it was when Covid-19 swept the earth. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola in Kenya: Wetin we know as di East African kontri record im first death - BBC

News Brief
Kenya has confirmed its first death from Ebola, marking the first known case of the disease in the country. The patient, a Kenyan citizen who had lived in the Democratic Republic of Congo for seven years, fell ill one month ago and was treated in DR Congo before arriving in Nairobi. Health Minister Aden Duale announced the death, stating the patient tested positive for the Bundibugyo species of Ebola virus. The patient died at 23:30 on Monday and was transferred to an isolation facility built during the Covid-19 period. At least 28 contacts have been identified, and authorities are tracing an additional 23 passengers and four crew members from the same flight. The Bundibugyo virus, which has caused over 4,082 deaths out of 8,463 cases in DR Congo since its emergence, remains highly infectious with no approved vaccine or treatment. The World Health Organization declared Kenya free from Ebola in August 2026, but the current outbreak is linked to DR Congo, where the virus is spreading rapidly. The situation highlights the ongoing challenges of managing Ebola outbreaks in regions with limited healthcare resources.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

刚果民主共和国难民营中转中心被烧毁,埃博拉应对工作面临挫折 - 联合国新闻

Ebola response faces setback as transit centre burns down in DR Congo camp - UN News
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 1 日,刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 伊图里省 Kigonze 营地的埃博拉患者转运中心被烧毁,导致该设施被废弃。这一事件使刚果民主共和国本已困难的埃博拉应对工作雪上加霜,自 2026 年 5 月中旬以来,暴力和冲突扰乱了刚果民主共和国的努力。暴发 已影响超过 8,000 人,导致 4,000 人死亡,伊图里省是此次疫情的震中。该地区的战斗延误了样本运输并限制了人道主义准入,增加了进一步传播的风险。联合国和世界卫生组织正在努力扩大埋葬能力,包括部署更多的埋葬队伍和床位,但情况仍然充满挑战。人道协调厅正在监测局势并敦促保护平民和流离失所者营地。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该事件发生于 2026 年 10 月 1 日,转运中心于周二晚上发生火灾。暴发 自 2026 年 5 月中旬以来一直持续,最近的冲突于 2026 年 9 月 27 日加剧。
地点与对象
该事件发生在刚果民主共和国(刚果民主共和国)伊图里省的 Kigonze 难民营。受影响人口包括流离失所者(主要是妇女和儿童)以及伊图里省(暴发 的震中)的广大人口。暴发 已遍布七个省,包括下韦莱省、上韦莱省、伊图里省、北基伍省、南基伍省、南乌班吉省和乔波省。
事件
伊图里省基贡泽难民营的埃博拉患者转运中心被烧毁,导致该设施被废弃。这一事件是刚果民主共和国应对埃博拉疫情所面临的持续挑战的一部分,暴力和冲突扰乱了努力。自 2026 年 5 月中旬以来,暴发 已导致 8,000 多例确诊病例和 4,000 例死亡。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已影响 8,000 多人,造成 4,000 人死亡。Kigonze 难民营的转运中心发生火灾,导致容量和获得护理的机会大幅减少。伊图里省和其他地区的战斗导致样本运输延误,人道主义援助准入受到限制,增加了病毒进一步传播的风险。世卫组织强调需要在高 传播 地区加强能力和干预。
应对、状态与不确定性
联合国和世界卫生组织正在努力保护卫生设施并提高能力,包括部署更多的埋葬队伍和床位。然而,局势仍在调查中,持续的冲突和不安全局势阻碍了应对工作。联合国援助协调办公室人道协调厅正在监测局势并呼吁保护平民和流离失所者营地。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola response faces setback as transit centre burns down in DR Congo camp - UN News

News Brief
A transit centre for Ebola patients in Kigonze camp, Ituri province, Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), burned down on 1 October 2026, causing the facility to be abandoned. This incident has worsened the already difficult Ebola response in the DRC, where violence and clashes have disrupted efforts since mid-May 2026. The outbreak has affected over 8,000 people, resulting in 4,000 deaths, with Ituri province being the epicentre. Fighting in the region has delayed sample transport and restricted humanitarian access, increasing the risk of further spread. The UN and WHO are working to expand capacity, including deploying more burial teams and beds, but the situation remains challenging. OCHA is monitoring the situation and urging protection of civilians and displacement camps.
Original source text (English)

Facebook Twitter Print Email Ebola response faces setback as transit centre burns down in DR Congo camp 1 October 2026 Peace and Security The enormous challenge of halting the spread of Ebola in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) just became even harder after a key transit centre for patients requiring treatment burned down in Ituri province. Condemning the blaze, the UN’s senior Ebola response official in DRC, Julien Harneis, also noted that Kigonze camp where the facility was open now lies abandoned. “It’s been a huge struggle to create Ebola transit centres [and] Ebola treatment centres; it’s required money, bringing in staff and it’s been a struggle all across the east,” Mr. Harneis told UN News. “So, when we lose a centre, that means we lose capacity, we lose investment and we have to start again. And above all, it means that people do not get access to the care that they really need.” Tweet URL Deserted shelters The camp had provided shelter for some 19,000 people fleeing violence in the resource-rich east of the country, close to the border with Rwanda. In recent days, thousands of people fled Kigonze following reports that soldiers had entered the site searching for weapons and people suspected of belonging to non-State armed groups. Gunfire was also reportedly heard. Describing the transit centre fire on Tuesday evening as “deeply disappointing”, Mr. Harneis explained that staff there had provided “essential and excellent” services to those who needed them. He added that the UN is working with authorities to ensure that health facilities are protected. In related developments, more than 3,000 people fled Rhoe displacement camp in Djugu territory (also in Ituri province) following clashes that intensified on 27 September. According to the UN aid coordination office, OCHA, at least four people were reportedly killed and seven injured, while several houses were burned down in Blukwa. Women and children account for most of those displaced, according to humanitarian sources. Fighting has also severely hampered the Ebola response and restricted humanitarian access to several health zones, including Ituri province’s Fataki, Drodro and Lita. Ongoing insecurity has also delayed the transport of laboratory samples from Fataki to Bunia and disrupted the transfer of confirmed Ebola patients for treatment. Some aid organizations have suspended activities in affected areas. Soundcloud North Kivu responders killed Meanwhile, in neighbouring North Kivu province, two people involved in the Ebola response were reportedly killed in Lubero on 27 and 28 September. Fighting in Masisi has displaced more than 24,000 people since mid-September. OCHA and humanitarian partners are monitoring the situation and calling on all parties to protect civilians, safeguard displacement camps and ensure humanitarian access. Violence has also been reported in South Kivu and Tanganyika provinces of DRC, forcing thousands more people from their homes and increasing humanitarian needs, the aid office said in an update earlier this week. A vast outbreak Ebola has spread across seven provinces since the public health emergency was declared in mid-May. It is the country’s 17th outbreak. The affected provinces are Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, Ituri, North Kivu, South Kivu, South Ubangi and Tshopo. More than 8,000 cases of infection have been confirmed including 4,000 deaths. Ituri remains the epicentre of the current emergency, with most cases in and around the cities of Bunia and Mongbwalu. In an update, the UN World Health Organization (WHO) stressed the double challenge for Ebola responders – including safe burial teams – who urgently need more help: “Where transmission remains intense, we need to increase capacity to halt the pace of infections. And in emerging hotspots, we need to intervene quickly to stop the virus from taking hold,” the agency said. How the UN is helping WHO is a key supporter of local health authorities, providing assistance with case management, surveillance, safe and dignified burials and community engagement. This is the case in Ituri’s Mongala health region, where 346 confirmed cases of Ebola have now been confirmed, with 149 deaths, since the first infection was detected on 18 May. Located 45 kilometres from the provincial capital, Bunia, access remains difficult and takes nearly two hours by car. Because Mongala lacks a treatment centre, patients are either referred to Nizi – 27 kilometres away – or Bunia, depending on where beds are available and the patient’s choice. In an update, WHO noted that the number of mobile safe and dignified burial teams has increased from 53 teams of 12 members to 114 teams deployed across Ituri province. In areas of high transmission, 57 community teams trained in safe and dignified burials were deployed across 11 health areas in Mongala and Fataki. The number of patient beds has increased too, with the aim of providing more than 1,800 beds across 54 health facilities in 34 health zones. “However, more than 1,400 additional health professionals are needed to support this expansion,” the agency stressed. “Increasing capacity allows us to admit more patients under better conditions and reduce delays. Rapid isolation and treatment of cases is essential,” said Dr Stéphane Mutombo, anaesthaesiologist and resuscitator, head of the clinical component within the holistic care pillar, for DRC’s Ministry of Health. This Ebola outbreak is caused by the Bundibugyo virus. It has become the largest and deadliest Ebola outbreak in DRC, surpassing the 2018–2020 emergency. It is second only to the 2014–2016 Ebola epidemic in West Africa, which infected more than 28,600 people and killed over 11,000 in Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone. Ebola Virus Disease Democratic Republic of the Congo Facebook Twitter Print Email Back to top
View five news elements
Time
The event occurred on 1 October 2026, with the transit centre fire taking place on Tuesday evening. The outbreak has been ongoing since mid-May 2026, and recent clashes intensified on 27 September 2026.
Location and population
The incident took place in Kigonze camp, Ituri province, Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). The affected population includes displaced people, primarily women and children, and the broader population in Ituri province, which has been the epicentre of the outbreak. The outbreak has spread across seven provinces, including Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, Ituri, North Kivu, South Kivu, South Ubangi, and Tshopo.
Event
A transit centre for Ebola patients in Kigonze camp, Ituri province, burned down, causing the facility to be abandoned. This incident is part of the ongoing challenges in the DRC's Ebola response, with violence and clashes disrupting efforts. The outbreak has resulted in over 8,000 confirmed cases and 4,000 deaths since mid-May 2026.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has affected over 8,000 people with 4,000 deaths. The transit centre fire in Kigonze camp has significantly reduced capacity and access to care. Fighting in Ituri province and other areas has delayed transport of samples and restricted humanitarian access, increasing the risk of further spread. The WHO has emphasized the need for more capacity and intervention in high-transmission areas.
Response, status and uncertainty
The UN and WHO are working to protect health facilities and increase capacity, including deploying more burial teams and beds. However, the situation remains under investigation, with ongoing clashes and insecurity hampering response efforts. The UN aid coordination office, OCHA, is monitoring the situation and calling for protection of civilians and displacement camps.

肯尼亚报告首例埃博拉患者从刚果民主共和国返回后死亡 - France 24

Kenya reports first-ever Ebola death after patient returns from DR Congo - France 24
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 6 日,肯尼亚报告了首例 埃博拉病毒病 死亡病例,此前一名居住在刚果民主共和国 (刚果民主共和国) 的肯尼亚国民返回该国后死亡。该患者在内罗毕医院被隔离,病毒检测呈阳性,并按照公共安全规定在 24 小时内被埋葬。肯尼亚是当前 暴发 中第四个记录埃博拉病例的国家,该疫情于 5 月中旬在刚果民主共和国东部开始。暴发 已在刚果民主共和国造成至少 8,400 例病例和 4,000 多人死亡,使其成为历史上增长最快的国家。肯尼亚卫生当局已确认 28 名接触者,其中包括家庭成员和卫生工作者,并对他们进行为期 21 天的监测。非洲疾病控制和预防中心赞扬肯尼亚的迅速反应,并派出一个团队支持遏制工作。该患者已在刚果民主共和国居住七年,在返回内罗毕前约一个月患病。这标志着病毒区域传播的重大发展,促使肯尼亚边境提高警惕。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 10 月 6 日,患者从刚果民主共和国返回后,肯尼亚报告了首例埃博拉死亡病例。该患者大约一个月前患病,并于 2026 年 10 月 4 日星期一晚去世。
地点与对象
该事件发生在肯尼亚,特别是内罗毕。受影响人群包括患者、其家人、卫生工作者以及卫生部确定的28名接触者。
事件
一名居住在刚果民主共和国的肯尼亚国民返回内罗毕并死亡后,肯尼亚确认了首例 埃博拉病毒病 病例。肯尼亚医学研究所证实,该国从未出现过埃博拉病毒暴发。
规模、影响与风险
这是肯尼亚首例 埃博拉病毒病 确诊病例和死亡病例。该患者病毒检测呈阳性,在内罗毕医院隔离后死亡。刚果民主共和国的 暴发 已记录至少 8,400 例病例和 4,000 多人死亡,使其成为历史上增长最快的 暴发。
应对、状态与不确定性
肯尼亚已启动接触者追踪工作,使边境点处于高度戒备状态,并正在监测其他病例。患者的接触者将被隔离 21 天。非洲疾病控制和预防中心对患者的快速识别和隔离表示赞赏,并派出高级别团队提供支持。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Kenya reports first-ever Ebola death after patient returns from DR Congo - France 24

News Brief
Kenya reported its first-ever death from Ebola virus disease on October 6, 2026, after a Kenyan national who had been living in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DR Congo) returned to the country and died. The patient, who had been isolated at Nairobi Hospital, tested positive for the virus and was buried within 24 hours following public safety protocols. Kenya is the fourth country to record an Ebola case in the current outbreak, which began in eastern DR Congo in mid-May. The outbreak has already caused at least 8,400 cases and over 4,000 deaths in DR Congo, making it the fastest-growing in history. Health authorities in Kenya have identified 28 contacts, including family members and health workers, and are monitoring them for 21 days. The Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention praised Kenya's swift response and sent a team to support containment efforts. The patient had been residing in DR Congo for seven years and fell ill about a month before returning to Nairobi. This marks a significant development in the regional spread of the virus, prompting heightened vigilance at Kenya's borders.

Kenya is the fourth country to record an Ebola case in the current outbreak, which was declared in eastern DR Congo in mid-May. Issued on: 06/10/2026 - 17:41Modified: 06/10/2026 - 18:10 3 min Reading time Share By: FRANCE 24 People with masks leave The Nairobi Hospital, where Kenya's first Ebola virus patient was treated and died, October 6, 2026. © Andrew Kasuku, AP A Kenyan citizen who had been living in Congo before returning to Nairobi over the weekend became Kenya’s first confirmed case of Ebola and the country’s first death from the disease in the fast-moving outbreak, authorities said Tuesday, sparking urgent contact-tracing efforts to try to contain the spread of the virus. The man had been residing in Congo for the past seven years and fell ill about a month ago. He was treated at various hospitals before travelling by road to Kampala, Uganda, where he boarded a flight to the Kenyan capital, arriving on Saturday afternoon, Health Minister Aden Duale said. Health authorities in Kenya have placed the country’s border points on high alert and are monitoring for possible additional cases. Duale said that the man had been isolated and treated at Nairobi Hospital, where he tested positive for the Ebola virus and died late Monday. Ebola is highly contagious and spreads in the human population through contact with bodily fluids such as vomit, blood or semen, and with contaminated surfaces and materials such as bedding and clothing. The current outbreak was declared in mid-May, months after it had been spreading in a remote part of eastern Congo. At least 8,400 cases have been recorded, including more than 4,000 deaths, according to government data, making it the fastest-growing outbreak of the disease in history. Kenya has never had an outbreak of Ebola, according to the Kenya Medical Research Institute. The country hosts a United States-funded isolation center for American citizens exposed to Ebola to quarantine for 21 days before travelling back to the US. The isolation facility faced legal battles and deadly protests by locals who feared it was exposing them to the virus. Kenya is the fourth country to record an Ebola case in the current outbreak, which was declared in eastern Congo in mid-May. Uganda has recorded 20 cases and France, one. Before Kenya reported its case, there had been no active cases outside Congo since Uganda declared its outbreak over in late August. Duale said that 28 contacts of the man who had returned on Saturday – including family members and health workers who cared for the patient – had been identified. Another 23 passengers on the flight and four crew members, who travelled on the same plane from Uganda, were being traced. The contacts will remain in isolation for 21 days and will be released after testing negative for the virus, said the ministry’s director-general, Dr. Patrick Amoth. After the man arrived in Nairobi, his relatives later took him in a private vehicle to Nairobi Hospital, where he was immediately isolated. He will be buried within 24 hours in accordance with recommended public safety protocols. The minister said that Kenya had tested 267 samples at five laboratories across the country since May before this first case was detected. The Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention on Tuesday commended the “rapid identification, isolation and testing of the patient” and said a high-level team had been dispatched to Kenya to offer support. In Congo, where the outbreak has spread to seven provinces, conditions are difficult. Responders have faced attacks on health teams, misinformation about the virus, a failure by locals to report symptoms and rebel violence that has limited access to treatment centres. While cases have slowed in Ituri province, the epicentre, the outbreak is spreading in emerging hot spots in North Kivu and South Kivu provinces. “It is like fighting a megafire. Multiple outbreaks are developing at the same time, with varying intensity and in different locations,” Stéphanie Hoffmann with Doctors Without Borders, known by its French acronym MSF, said in a statement. The World Health Organisation has warned that it could surpass the deadliest Ebola outbreak in history, which erupted more than a decade ago and killed more than 11,000 people in West Africa. (FRANCE 24 with AP) Advertising Keywords for this article Kenya Ebola Public health DR Congo virus Page not found The content you requested does not exist or is not available anymore

The Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention commended the rapid identification and isolation of the patient and sent a high-level team for support.

随着刚果民​​主共和国病毒“激增”,肯尼亚确认首例埃博拉病例 - 《卫报》

Kenya confirms first Ebola case as virus ‘surges’ in DR Congo province - The Guardian
新闻简报肯尼亚于 2026 年 10 月 2 日确认首例埃博拉病例,此前一名在刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 生活了七年的男子死亡。该男子于 10 月 3 日抵达肯尼亚,抵达后即被隔离,病毒检测呈阳性。他的死亡标志着肯尼亚首次报道埃博拉死亡病例。刚果民主共和国的病例激增,截至 10 月 2 日,北基伍省占新增感染病例的 40%。自5月开始的暴发已蔓延至七个省份,该国面临冲突、对当局不信任和资源缺乏等挑战。刚果民主共和国卫生部长确认了 28 名接触者,迄今为止只有 1 次检测确诊感染。官员们正在为同一航班上的其他乘客和机组人员准备隔离安排。目前情况仍在调查中,目前尚无适用于 Bundibugyo 菌株的 疫苗 或特定 治疗。无国界医生组织指出,由于不稳定和设施有限,应对工作变得更加复杂。
查看新闻五要素
时间
一名于 10 月 3 日从刚果民主共和国抵达的男子死亡后,肯尼亚于 2026 年 10 月 2 日确认了第一例埃博​​拉病例。刚果民主共和国的 暴发 于 2026 年 5 月首次宣布,截至 10 月 2 日,8,463 例确诊病例中已报告 4,082 例死亡。
地点与对象
The case occurred in Kenya, specifically in Nairobi. The affected population includes the man who died, his 28 contacts, and potentially 23 passengers and 4 crew members on the same flight. The DRC outbreak spans seven provinces in the country’s north and east, with North Kivu province being particularly affected, home to around 2 million people.
事件
2026 年 10 月 2 日,肯尼亚确认首例埃博拉死亡病例,此前一名在刚果民主共和国生活了七年的男子返回肯尼亚后死亡。卫生部长亚丁·杜阿勒 (Aden Duale) 表示,该男子抵达后已被隔离,并确诊病毒呈阳性。刚果民主共和国的病例激增,截至 10 月 2 日,北基伍省占新增病例的 40%。
规模、影响与风险
自 2026 年 5 月 暴发 开始以来,刚果民主共和国已报告 8,463 例确诊病例和 4,082 例死亡。该病毒的 Bundibugyo 毒株是罪魁祸首,并且没有可用的 疫苗 或特定的 治疗。在肯尼亚,已确定 28 名接触者,迄今为止只有 1 人检测结果呈阳性。暴发已蔓延至刚果民主共和国七个省份,该地区面临冲突、资源匮乏、埃博拉患者治疗设施不足等挑战。
应对、状态与不确定性
肯尼亚卫生部长证实了这一病例,并表示该男子已被隔离并接受检测。官员正在调查同一航班上的另外 23 名乘客和 4 名机组人员。正在为高危人群做好隔离安排。刚果民主共和国的应对措施因不稳定、冲突和资源短缺而受到阻碍,无国界医生组织指出需要更多床位和提高护理标准。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Kenya confirms first Ebola case as virus ‘surges’ in DR Congo province - The Guardian

News Brief
Kenya confirmed its first-ever Ebola case on 2 October 2026, following the death of a man who had been living in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) for seven years. The man, who arrived in Kenya on 3 October, was isolated upon arrival and tested positive for the virus. His death marks the first reported Ebola fatality in Kenya. The DRC has experienced a surge in cases, with North Kivu province accounting for 40% of new infections as of 2 October. The outbreak, which began in May, has spread to seven provinces, and the country faces challenges such as conflict, mistrust of authorities, and a lack of resources. The DRC’s health minister identified 28 contacts, with only one test so far confirming infection. Officials are preparing quarantine arrangements for additional passengers and crew members on the same flight. The situation remains under investigation, with no vaccine or specific treatment available for the Bundibugyo strain. The response has been complicated by instability and limited facilities, as noted by Médecins Sans Frontières.

Photograph: Daniel Irungu/EPA View image in fullscreen Volunteer health workers at an Ebola emergency response training session conducted by Médecins Sans Frontières in Ongata Rongai, Kenya, in August. Photograph: Daniel Irungu/EPA Kenya confirms first Ebola case as virus ‘surges’ in DR Congo province Health minister says 28 contacts identified after death of citizen who arrived from DRC on Saturday Kenya has reported its first-ever Ebola death, the health minister announced on Tuesday, as the disease continues to spread rapidly through the north-east of the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), where the outbreak was announced in May. This is the 17th outbreak of the highly infectious, often deadly disease in the DRC. As of 2 October, 4,082 people had died out of 8,463 confirmed cases in the country since the emergence of the Bundibugyo strain of the Ebola virus, for which there is still no vaccine nor specific treatment. A Kenyan man who had been living in the DRC for seven years died after returning to Kenya, the Kenyan health minister Aden Duale told reporters. The man, who has not been named, had been treated in the DRC after falling ill about a month ago. He then travelled overland to the Ugandan capital, Kampala, before flying to Nairobi on 3 October. Kenya has previously never reported a case of Ebola, but neighbouring Uganda recorded 20 – almost all people who had travelled from the DRC – and two deaths, before being declared Ebola-free in August. Duale said the man was taken by a relative and a friend from Nairobi’s international airport to Nairobi hospital, where he was immediately isolated, suffering from “fever, chills, intense fatigue, weakness, muscle pain, painful swallowing, sore throat and bleeding under the skin at the injection sites”. He said the man was subsequently screened and confirmed positive for the virus, but “regrettably passed on last night at around 1130” in the evening (2030 GMT). Duale said officials had listed 28 contacts of the deceased, including family members and healthcare workers, but that so far “only one test has turned positive and is the one that we have just discussed”. He added that authorities were looking for another 23 passengers and four crew members who were on the same flight with the man and that “arrangements” for quarantine for those at risk had been prepared. “I wish to assure the public not to panic, as all systems are in place to mitigate the spread of the disease,” he said. Duale said a “safe and dignified burial” of the man had been planned for later in the day, in line with public health standards, but did not give further details. Kenyans reacted furiously to government plans earlier this year to allow the US to set up an Ebola facility at Laikipia airbase, about 125 miles from Nairobi. After starting in the DRC’s north-eastern Ituri province, the current outbreak has spread to seven provinces in the country’s north and east. DRC politician beaten to death after radio appearance about Ebola outbreak Read more The response to the virus in the vast central African country has been hindered by instability, including clashes between armed groups, mistrust of authorities – which has led to violence against health workers and other officials – and a lack of resources amid huge cuts in international aid. The medical charity Médecins Sans Frontières warned on Monday of an “alarming surge” in cases in eastern North Kivu province, which borders Uganda. The province now accounts for 40% of all new Ebola cases, compared with 24% at the end of August, the NGO said. “It is like fighting a megafire,” said Stephanie Hoffmann, coordinator at MSF’s Ebola treatment centre in the city of Butembo. “Multiple outbreaks are developing at the same time, with varying intensity and in different locations.” The wider Butembo health zone is home to around 2 million people and only has four Ebola treatment centres, two of which only opened recently, MSF said. Hoffman said: “Because there are not enough beds available, we are sometimes forced to refer confirmed Ebola patients elsewhere, despite the significant risk this poses to the wider community. In addition, standards of care in some peripheral facilities do not always meet the requirements for Ebola treatment.” North Kivu is particularly affected by conflict, with 134 violent incidents there in the past month, according to tracking group Armed Conflict Location and Event Data, more than any other province. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola: What are the symptoms, how does it spread and where did it come from? - The Star, Kenya

News Brief
The article from The Star, Kenya, published on 2026-10-06, provides an overview of Ebola virus disease, focusing on its origins, symptoms, transmission, and prevention. It highlights that the virus was first discovered in 1976 near the Ebola river in northern DRC and was named after the river. The disease is zoonotic, with bats being the most likely reservoir. Symptoms include fever, severe headache, muscle and joint pain, weakness, and unexplained haemorrhaging, typically appearing 8-10 days after infection. The article notes that the disease is now treatable with vaccines like Inmazeb and Ebanga, approved by the US Food and Drug Administration in 2020, and is available through the World Health Organization during outbreaks. It also emphasizes the importance of behavioral prevention measures, such as avoiding contact with bodily fluids and infected individuals, and the need for local production of diagnostics and treatments to ensure accessibility in endemic regions. The article underscores that while Ebola is deadly, it is preventable and curable with the right interventions.
Original source text (English)
Ebola outbreaks emerge periodically in several African countries, most notably in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). Public health expert Yap Boum, who has been involved in managing Ebola outbreaks in the past, answers some key questions about Ebola’s history, treatment, and how to keep safe. What are the origins of the virus? Ebola is an old and deadly disease that was discovered in 1976 near the Ebola river in northern DRC. The virus was named after the river. It was discovered by scientists including Jean-Jacques Muyembe – a Congolese microbiologist and general director of the DRC Institut National pour la Recherche Biomedicale – and researchers from the Institute of Tropical Medicine, including Stefaan Pattyn, Guido van der Groen and Peter Piot. Professor Muyembe was called to the village of Yambuku in northern Zaire (now DRC) where a mysterious illness had broken out. He took a sample and sent it to the Institute of Tropical Medicine laboratory in Belgium, where the virus was isolated. Since then, there have been five identified Ebola virus strains, four of which are known to cause disease in humans: Ebola virus (Zaire ebolavirus); Sudan virus (Sudan ebolavirus); Taï Forest virus (Taï Forest ebolavirus, formerly Côte d'Ivoire ebolavirus); and Bundibugyo virus (Bundibugyo ebolavirus). It’s a zoonotic disease (animal-borne) though the natural reservoir host of Ebola virus remains unknown. However, bats are the most likely reservoir. What are the symptoms of Ebola? While the signs and symptoms may appear between 2 and 21 days after contact with the virus, they usually appear between 8 and 10 days. They are quite similar to many tropical diseases, especially malaria and typhoid fever, with which they share symptoms such as: - fever - aches and pains, such as severe headache and muscle and joint pain - weakness and fatigue - sore throat - loss of appetite - abdominal pain - diarrhoea and vomiting - unexplained haemorrhaging, bleeding or bruising. The main differences appear in the late stages of infection. These symptoms might include red eyes, skin rash and hiccups. Can it be treated? The Ebola virus disease can now be treated. The PALM clinical trial – implemented between 2018 and 2020 in the DRC – has evaluated four drug candidates. Two of them – Inmazeb and Ebanga – were approved by the US Food and Drug Administration in October and December 2020 to treat the Ebola virus disease caused by the Ebola virus. They are made available to patients by the World Health Organization (WHO) during Ebola outbreaks and aren’t available in the market. Inmazeb is a combination of three monoclonal antibodies and Ebanga is a single monoclonal antibody. Monoclonal antibodies (often abbreviated as mAbs) are proteins produced in a lab or other manufacturing facility that act like natural antibodies to stop a germ such as a virus from replicating after it has infected a person. Ebanga was isolated from a human survivor of the 1995 Ebola outbreak in Kikwit in the DRC who maintained circulating antibodies against the Ebola virus for more than a decade after infection. Without treatment the average case fatality rate is approximately 50%. But it has ranged from 25% to 90% in past outbreaks. Can it be prevented? People can be vaccinated against one strain, the Zaire Ebola virus. It became a preventable disease following the validation of one vaccine candidate during the Ca Suffit Ebola clinical trial in 2015 in Guinea. The Ebola vaccine rVSV-ZEBOV (called Ervebo®) was approved by the US Food and Drug Administration in December 2019. This vaccine is given as a single dose vaccine and has been found to be safe and protective with a reported 100% efficacy. Though it has not yet been approved by the US Food and Drug Administration, Johnson & Johnson has a two-dose vaccine for the Zaire strain that was used under emergency use in 2019 during an Ebola outbreak in the DRC. This vaccine requires an initial dose and a “booster” dose 56 days later and could be made available through the WHO during outbreaks. How can you protect yourself? Ebola is a highly transmissible disease but, aside from vaccination for the Zaire strain, its spread can be prevented through behavioural measures. People should avoid contact with blood and body fluids – such as urine, faeces, saliva, sweat, vomit, breast milk, amniotic fluid, semen and vaginal fluids – of people who are sick. People should also avoid contact with their personal items which might have traces of these fluids. People who were previously infected can still carry the disease in their semen. Because it’s a zoonotic disease – that is, transmitted from animal to humans – people should avoid contact with bats, forest antelopes, nonhuman primates (such as monkeys and chimpanzees) and wild meat and blood especially in endemic areas. Lastly, funeral or burial practices that involve touching the body of someone who may have died from Ebola should be avoided. Experience in West Africa shows these burial practices to be among the super spreaders of the Ebola virus. Ebola virus disease is a deadly disease that is preventable and curable. The next step is the local production of diagnostics, vaccines and drugs to ensure that endemic countries control their own stock and can make them available to their population. Africa can’t be left behind once more when it comes to diagnostics, vaccines and treatment, as it has been during the COVID pandemic and monkeypox outbreaks. Yap Boum, Professor in the faculty of Medicine, Mbarara University of Science and Technology This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola kills first victim in Kenya as deadly disease hits FOURTH country after infected patient boarded flight from Congo

News Brief
Kenya has confirmed its first-ever case of Ebola virus disease, marking the fourth country affected by the outbreak. The patient, an unnamed man who had been living in the Democratic Republic of Congo for seven years, died in Nairobi on October 5, 2026, after traveling from Congo. Health officials are tracing 23 passengers and four crew members from a flight that connected Uganda to Nairobi, as well as 28 additional contacts, including relatives and medics. The Bundibugyo strain of Ebola, which has no approved vaccine or treatment, was identified as the cause. The outbreak in Congo, which has already killed over 4,000 people, is described as the fastest-growing epidemic on record. Officials have placed Kenya’s border points on high alert and urged people not to panic. The World Health Organization has warned that the crisis could surpass the 2014-16 West African epidemic in scale, highlighting the need for urgent containment measures.
Original source text (English)
EBOLA has killed its first victim in Kenya as the deadly disease hits its fourth country after an infected passenger boarded a flight from Congo. The Kenyan man died in Nairobi late on Monday after travelling from the Democratic Republic of Congo where the virus has already killed more than 4,000 people. Ebola has killed its first victim as the deadly disease hits its third country Credit: AFP Health Minister Aden Duale confirmed Kenya’s first ever case on Tuesday Credit: Shutterstock Editorial His death marks Kenya’s first-ever recorded case of Ebola and has sparked an urgent hunt for dozens of people who may have come into contact with him. Health chiefs are now scrambling to trace 23 passengers and four crew members who were on the same flight from Uganda – a connecting flight to Nairobi – on Saturday. Sign up for The US Sun newsletter Thank you! Another 28 contacts – including relatives and medics who cared for the victim – have already been identified and face 21 days of isolation. Health Minister Aden Duale said that so far “only one test has turned out positive,” referring to the dead unnamed man from Kenya . Kenyan border points have also been placed on high alert amid fears the highly contagious killer could spread further. Despite the dramatic escalation, Duale urged terrified Kenyans “not to panic, as all systems are in place to mitigate the spread of the disease”. It comes as the outbreak rips through eastern Congo at alarming speed, with health workers battling what one expert described as a viral “megafire”. At least 8,463 cases have now been confirmed in Congo, with 4,082 deaths – making it the country’s largest ever recorded Ebola outbreak. People put on masks at the entrance of The Nairobi Hospital where Kenya’s first Ebola virus patient was treated and died More than 4000 people have already died from the virus in Congo Credit: Reuters The crisis has already spilled beyond its borders, with Uganda previously recording 20 cases and two deaths before being declared Ebola-free in August. France has also recorded a case during the current outbreak. Kenya had never previously recorded an Ebola case, according to the Kenya Medical Research Institute. Duale said the unnamed Kenyan victim had been living in Congo for seven years and first fell ill around a month ago. He was treated at several hospitals there before making the gruelling journey by road to the Ugandan capital Kampala. From there, he boarded a flight to Nairobi – Kenya’s capital – and landed on Saturday afternoon despite already being ill. The patient passed routine screening at Nairobi airport, with officials saying medication he was taking may have masked his symptoms. A relative and friend then drove him to Nairobi Hospital, where doctors immediately isolated him. More people spotted wearing masks outside the hospital where the first Kenyan victim died Health chiefs are now scrambling to trace 23 passengers and four crew members Credit: Reuters Duale said he was suffering from “fever, chills, intense fatigue, weakness, muscle pain, painful swallowing, sore throat and bleeding under the skin at the injection sites”. Doctors feared he was suffering from a vital haemorrhagic fever and rushed samples away for testing. They came back positive for the Bundibugyo species of Ebola. But the patient’s condition deteriorated and he died at around 11.30pm on Monday. Duale said: “[The victim] regrettably passed on last night at around 1130.” The UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA) has announced it is expanding its wastewater surveillance programme to now detect “dangerous” infections such as Crimean-Congo haemorrhagic fever, Marburg virus, Ebola and Lassa fever. Ebola can tear through communities through direct contact with infected bodily fluids including blood and vomit, while contaminated bedding and clothing can also spread the virus. Victims can initially suffer fever, crushing muscle pain, headaches and sore throats before developing vomiting, diarrhoea and, in severe cases, internal and external bleeding. Kenya had never previously recorded an Ebola case, according to the Kenya Medical Research Institute Credit: AP Duale said the unnamed Kenyan victim had been living in Congo for seven years Credit: Reuters The Bundibugyo strain behind the current catastrophe is relatively rare and has no approved vaccine or specific treatment. The Kenyan case comes as scientists say the deadly outbreak was scientists silently spreading for months before the alarm was finally raised. The epidemic was officially declared in Congo on May 15, but research published in the journal Science indicated it may have begun in January – or even earlier. Researchers uncovered more than 500 suspected cases from the months before the outbreak was formally recognised after interviewing doctors, nurses, survivors and grieving families. Earlier this year, authorities said last week that the Ebola outbreak was the fastest-growing epidemic on record . And now, it is also the world’s second-largest outbreak on record, according to government data. The virus has since ripped through seven provinces as exhausted health teams battle rebel violence, mistrust and attacks on responders. North Kivu, which borders Uganda, has emerged as an increasingly dangerous hotspot. Médecins Sans Frontières warned on Monday of an “alarming surge” there, with the province now accounting for almost 40 per cent of newly confirmed cases nationwide . Stephanie Hoffmann, coordinator at MSF’s Ebola treatment centre in Butembo, warned: “It is like fighting a megafire. Multiple outbreaks are developing at the same time, with varying intensity and in different locations.” The wider Butembo health zone is home to around two million people but has just four Ebola treatment centres, according to MSF. Hoffmann warned the shortage of beds was creating another frightening danger. France has also recorded a case during the current outbreak Credit: EPA Earlier this year, authorities said last week that the Ebola outbreak was the fastest-growing epidemic on record Credit: Reuters She said: “Because there are not enough beds available, we are sometimes forced to refer confirmed Ebola patients elsewhere, despite the significant risk this poses to the wider community.” The chaos is being compounded by brutal fighting in eastern Congo, where armed groups have made it increasingly difficult for medical teams to reach patients and contain infections. Health chiefs have also warned that the dead can remain highly infectious, meaning the movement of victims’ bodies for funerals and burials risks fuelling further transmission. The World Health Organisation has raised concerns that the crisis could eventually surpass the devastating 2014-16 West African epidemic , which killed more than 11,000 people. WHO pandemic threat chief Maria Van Kerkhove warned there was still “intense transmission” and said “the scale of the response is not where we need it to be”. She said: “We have 900 beds right now and we are scaling up to 3000, at least. “We are looking at a massive scale-up of clinical care. Having Ebola treatment centres in areas where we know there is active transmission, but also where there are new cases.” But despite the spiralling emergency in Africa, Van Kerkhove stressed that “the risk to people globally is low”

Time
The first case was reported on Monday, October 5, 2026, with the patient's death occurring on the same day. The outbreak in Congo was officially declared on May 15, 2026, but may have started as early as January 2026, according to research published in the journal Science.
Location and population
Kenya, specifically Nairobi, and the Democratic Republic of Congo, particularly the North Kivu province which borders Uganda. The outbreak has affected at least seven provinces in Congo and involves a population of around two million in the Butembo health zone.
Event
Kenya confirmed its first-ever case of Ebola virus disease, with a man who had been living in Congo for seven years and had fallen ill a month prior. The case was confirmed by Health Minister Aden Duale, and the patient was treated at Nairobi Hospital before dying. The Bundibugyo species of Ebola was identified as the cause.
Scale, impact and risk
At least 8,463 cases and 4,082 deaths have been confirmed in Congo, making it the country’s largest ever recorded Ebola outbreak. The patient’s death has prompted an urgent contact tracing effort, identifying 23 passengers and four crew members on the flight from Uganda to Nairobi, as well as 28 additional contacts. The Bundibugyo strain has no approved vaccine or specific treatment, and the WHO has warned that the outbreak could surpass the 2014-16 West African epidemic in scale.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health officials are tracing contacts and isolating them for 21 days. Kenya has placed border points on high alert, and the UK Health Security Agency is expanding its wastewater surveillance to detect Ebola. The situation remains under investigation, with the WHO and MSF expressing concerns about the outbreak's potential to grow further.

联合国儿童基金会:“埃博拉夺走了儿童所依赖的人” - 联合国新闻

‘Ebola takes the people children depend on’: UNICEF - UN News
新闻简报联合国儿童基金会于 2026 年 10 月 6 日发布的报告强调了刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 东部埃博拉病毒 暴发 对儿童造成的破坏性影响。自 5 月宣布 暴发 以来,已有 1,900 多名儿童失去父母一方或双方或照顾者,其中 1,581 名儿童在北基伍省,322 名儿童在邻近地区。截至9月29日,至少有2,017名儿童被确诊感染埃博拉病毒,其中1,062人死亡。五岁以下儿童面临的风险特别高,死亡率为十分之七。联合国儿童基金会正在支持伊图里和北基伍省的托儿所照顾受影响的儿童,强调早期发现和 治疗 的必要性。与此同时,肯尼亚确认了首例埃博拉病例和死亡病例,很可能是从刚果民主共和国输入的,世界卫生组织正在努力收集更多信息。该报告强调了儿童在 暴发 期间面临的情感和后勤挑战。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告于2026年10月6日发布。暴发于2026年5月宣布,截至2026年9月29日,至少有2,017名儿童被确诊感染埃博拉病毒,其中至少1,062人死亡。
地点与对象
暴发 位于刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 东部,特别是北基伍省及其邻近地区。受影响人口包括失去父母、照顾者或与父母失散的儿童,其中北基伍省有 1,581 名儿童,邻近地区有 322 名儿童。超过 1,900 名儿童受到影响。
事件
联合国儿童基金会的报告强调了埃博拉病毒 暴发 对刚果民主共和国儿童的影响,指出许多人失去了父母或照顾者,有些人死于这种疾病。报告还提到肯尼亚确认首例埃博拉病例和死亡病例,病例可能来自刚果民主共和国。
规模、影响与风险
As of 29 September 2026, at least 2,017 children had been confirmed with Ebola in the DRC, with at least 1,062 deaths. Children under five have a death rate of seven in 10, up from six in 10. The report emphasizes the need for early detection and care for children affected by the disease.
应对、状态与不确定性
联合国儿童基金会支持伊图里和北基伍省的 12 家托儿所,照顾接触病毒的儿童或其父母在 治疗 的儿童。该组织呼吁在刚果民主共和国的每个埃博拉 治疗 中心设立更多托儿所。世界卫生组织正在监测确认首例病例和死亡的肯尼亚的情况,并正在与当局合作以获取更多详细信息。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

‘Ebola takes the people children depend on’: UNICEF - UN News

News Brief
The UNICEF report, published on 6 October 2026, highlights the devastating impact of the Ebola outbreak in the eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) on children. Since the outbreak was declared in May, over 1,900 children have either lost one or both parents or caregivers, with 1,581 in North Kivu and 322 in neighboring areas. As of 29 September, at least 2,017 children had been confirmed with Ebola, and 1,062 had died. Children under five face a particularly high risk, with a death rate of seven in 10. UNICEF is supporting nurseries in Ituri and North Kivu to care for affected children, emphasizing the need for early detection and treatment. Meanwhile, Kenya confirmed its first case and death from Ebola, likely imported from the DRC, with the WHO working to gather more information. The report underscores the emotional and logistical challenges faced by children during the outbreak.
Original source text (English)

Facebook Twitter Print Email ‘Ebola takes the people children depend on’: UNICEF 6 October 2026 Health The Ebola outbreak in the eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) is robbing children of their loved ones – with some succumbing to the deadly disease themselves, the UN Children’s Fund (UNICEF) reported on Tuesday. The update comes as regional neighbour Kenya confirmed its first case and death from the disease, believed to have come from DRC. “Ebola takes the people children depend on and unravels their lives,” said Halimatou Amadou, UNICEF Chief of Communication in the DRC, speaking to journalists in Geneva. “The parents fall ill, a child may be exposed, the parent is taken for treatment or dies. At the very moment a child most needs someone, fear and stigma can keep relatives and neighbours away.” Nearly 2,000 children affected More than 1,900 boys and girls have either lost one or both parents, or their caregivers, since the outbreak was declared in May, or have been separated from them. Most of these children, 1,581, are in North Kivu province, the epicentre of the crisis. A further 322 are in neighbouring North Kivu province. “And these are the children we know about because these figures cover only the area where UNICEF works with social affairs teams in the affected provinces,” she said. Fighters and survivors Ms. Amadou put names behind the numbers, such as Lina*, a seven-month-old girl who she met at a UNICEF-supported nursery next to an Ebola treatment centre in Bunia, the Ituri capital. Lina’s mother died and her father is missing. Her grandfather, who lives nearby, has visited twice and the hope is that they will soon be together. Another child, Raphael*, just eight months old, survived Ebola but lost eight family members. He stayed at the nursery for more than three weeks until his older brother could take him home. Young lives lost “Children are not only losing their parents. They're also losing their own lives,” Ms. Amadou said. As of 29 September, at least 2,017 children had been confirmed with Ebola in DRC. At least 1,062 have died. She noted that children make up just over one in four confirmed cases, but around one in three deaths. However, the situation is worse among under-fives where the death rate is seven in 10, up from six in 10 just a few weeks ago. “Many die before the virus is ever detected, before they ever reach treatment,” she said. Care and access While in Bunia Ms. Amadou “also saw what works” for young lives upended by Ebola. UNICEF supports 12 nurseries in Ituri and North Kivyu that care for babies and young children who have been exposed to the virus, or whose parents are in treatment, or who are on their own. “Children are fed, they are held, they're cared for, they are also watched closely so that if symptoms appear, they get paediatric care fast,” she said. UNICEF is calling for nurseries at every Ebola treatment centre in the DRC, while underscoring the need to maintain health essential services. That way children receive early detection and quick referral to Ebola care, as well as protection from common childhood diseases. First case in Kenya Meanwhile, the World Health Organization (WHO) is monitoring the situation in Kenya, which on Tuesday confirmed its first case and fatality, according to media reports. Spokesman Christian Lindmeier the case was “likely imported from DRC” but more information was not available. He said colleagues on the ground are working with the Kenyan authorities to get more details. *Names have been changed to protect the children’s privacy and identity Ebola Virus Disease Facebook Twitter Print Email Back to top
View five news elements
Time
The report was published on 6 October 2026. The outbreak was declared in May 2026, and as of 29 September 2026, at least 2,017 children had been confirmed with Ebola, with at least 1,062 deaths.
Location and population
The outbreak is in the eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically in North Kivu province and its neighboring areas. The affected population includes children who have lost parents, caregivers, or been separated from them, with 1,581 children in North Kivu and 322 in neighboring regions. Over 1,900 children have been impacted.
Event
The UNICEF report highlights the impact of the Ebola outbreak on children in the DRC, noting that many have lost their parents or caregivers, and some have died from the disease. The report also mentions Kenya confirming its first case and death from Ebola, likely imported from the DRC.
Scale, impact and risk
As of 29 September 2026, at least 2,017 children had been confirmed with Ebola in the DRC, with at least 1,062 deaths. Children under five have a death rate of seven in 10, up from six in 10. The report emphasizes the need for early detection and care for children affected by the disease.
Response, status and uncertainty
UNICEF supports 12 nurseries in Ituri and North Kivu to care for children exposed to the virus or whose parents are in treatment. The organization is calling for more nurseries at every Ebola treatment centre in the DRC. The WHO is monitoring the situation in Kenya, where the first case and death were confirmed, and is working with authorities to obtain more details.
